From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally got me to come in is that I just don’t really care about things I used to enjoy. Sailing races, my Saturday shift at the historical society, even working on projects in my woodshop… they all just feel like chores now, and I keep putting them off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad, yeah… but it’s more like this constant low-grade dread and guilt. I keep thinking I should’ve seen one more risk coming, even though people tell me the controls were in place. It just kind of follows me around most days.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to find pleasure in the things you used to enjoy, even if you don't feel like doing them?\n\nPatient: Not really. Even when I make myself show up or start something, it doesn’t feel enjoyable the way it used to. The sailing races, the historical society shift, working in the woodshop… they used to help keep me steady, and now they mostly just feel like tasks I’m avoiding.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel happy or excited about anything at all?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. My wife and kids can be laughing at dinner, or I can finish some difficult milestone at work, and I know those are things that should feel good. But most of the time I just feel kind of flat… like I’m watching it from a little distance instead of really feeling happy or excited.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Since the scaffold accident, during that business readjustment. So… a little over a year now, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or things that you still find pleasure in, no matter how small they might seem?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something, but honestly… not really. The things that used to keep me balanced—sailing races, my Saturday shift at the historical society, even tinkering in the woodshop—they just feel like chores now. I keep putting them off instead of looking forward to them.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of dread and guilt, and the loss of interest in activities, been constant over the past year or have you had periods where you felt better?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty constant, I’d say. I don’t really remember any stretch where I felt back to normal. Since that scaffold accident during the business readjustment… it’s just been there, a little over a year now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past year where you felt a little better and actually enjoyed something?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I might get through a day a little easier here or there, but actually enjoying something… I can’t say I have. That’s what finally got me to come in, honestly. Sailing, the historical society on Saturdays, working in the woodshop—those used to help me feel steady, and now they just feel like chores I keep avoiding.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in enjoyment when spending time with your wife and kids, or do you still find pleasure in being with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that. I still love them, of course, and I want to be there, but it’s different. My wife and kids can be laughing at dinner, and I know I should feel happy in that moment, but mostly I just feel kind of flat, like I’m watching it from a distance. Same with work—if I finish a hard milestone, I know it should feel good, but it just doesn’t really land.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

My wife and kids can be laughing at dinner, or I can finish some difficult milestone at work, and I know those are things that should feel good. But most of the time I just feel kind of flat… like I’m watching it from a little distance instead of really feeling happy or excited.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Since the scaffold accident, during that business readjustment. So… a little over a year now, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or things that you still find pleasure in, no matter how small they might seem?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something, but honestly… not really. The things that used to keep me balanced—sailing races, my Saturday shift at the historical society, even tinkering in the woodshop—they just feel like chores now. I keep putting them off instead of looking forward to them.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of dread and guilt, and the loss of interest in activities, been constant over the past year or have you had periods where you felt better?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty constant, I’d say. I don’t really remember any stretch where I felt back to normal. Since that scaffold accident during the business readjustment… it’s just been there, a little over a year now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past year where you felt a little better and actually enjoyed something?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I might get through a day a little easier here or there, but actually enjoying something… I can’t say I have. That’s what finally got me to come in, honestly. Sailing, the historical society on Saturdays, working in the woodshop—those used to help me feel steady, and now they just feel like chores I keep avoiding.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in enjoyment when spending time with your wife and kids, or do you still find pleasure in being with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that. I still love them, of course, and I want to be there, but it’s different. My wife and kids can be laughing at dinner, and I know I should feel happy in that moment, but mostly I just feel kind of flat, like I’m watching it from a distance. Same with work—if I finish a hard milestone, I know it should feel good, but it just doesn’t really land.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how you've been feeling over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Not much change, honestly. It’s been pretty much the same since the scaffold accident during that business readjustment—so, a little over a year now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your feelings of dread and guilt have gotten worse over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say they’ve stayed with me more than gotten sharply worse. It’s this low-level dread and guilt that follows me around. I keep thinking I should’ve seen one more risk coming, even though people tell me the controls were in place. It’s just hard to let that go.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your feelings over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: Over the past few weeks, not really, no. It’s been about the same. This all kind of started after the scaffold accident during the business readjustment, and it’s been going on a little over a year now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally got me to come in is that I just don’t really care about things I used to enjoy. Sailing races, my Saturday shift at the historical society, even working on projects in my woodshop… they all just feel like chores now, and I keep putting them off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad, yeah… but it’s more like this constant low-grade dread and guilt. I keep thinking I should’ve seen one more risk coming, even though people tell me the controls were in place. It just kind of follows me around most days.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to find pleasure in the things you used to enjoy, even if you don't feel like doing them?\n\nPatient: Not really. Even when I make myself show up or start something, it doesn’t feel enjoyable the way it used to. The sailing races, the historical society shift, working in the woodshop… they used to help keep me steady, and now they mostly just feel like tasks I’m avoiding.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel happy or excited about anything at all?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. My wife and kids can be laughing at dinner, or I can finish some difficult milestone at work, and I know those are things that should feel good. But most of the time I just feel kind of flat… like I’m watching it from a little distance instead of really feeling happy or excited.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Since the scaffold accident, during that business readjustment. So… a little over a year now, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or things that you still find pleasure in, no matter how small they might seem?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something, but honestly… not really. The things that used to keep me balanced—sailing races, my Saturday shift at the historical society, even tinkering in the woodshop—they just feel like chores now. I keep putting them off instead of looking forward to them.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of dread and guilt, and the loss of interest in activities, been constant over the past year or have you had periods where you felt better?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty constant, I’d say. I don’t really remember any stretch where I felt back to normal. Since that scaffold accident during the business readjustment… it’s just been there, a little over a year now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past year where you felt a little better and actually enjoyed something?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I might get through a day a little easier here or there, but actually enjoying something… I can’t say I have. That’s what finally got me to come in, honestly. Sailing, the historical society on Saturdays, working in the woodshop—those used to help me feel steady, and now they just feel like chores I keep avoiding.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in enjoyment when spending time with your wife and kids, or do you still find pleasure in being with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that. I still love them, of course, and I want to be there, but it’s different. My wife and kids can be laughing at dinner, and I know I should feel happy in that moment, but mostly I just feel kind of flat, like I’m watching it from a distance. Same with work—if I finish a hard milestone, I know it should feel good, but it just doesn’t really land.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how you've been feeling over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Not much change, honestly. It’s been pretty much the same since the scaffold accident during that business readjustment—so, a little over a year now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your feelings of dread and guilt have gotten worse over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say they’ve stayed with me more than gotten sharply worse. It’s this low-level dread and guilt that follows me around. I keep thinking I should’ve seen one more risk coming, even though people tell me the controls were in place. It’s just hard to let that go.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your feelings over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: Over the past few weeks, not really, no. It’s been about the same. This all kind of started after the scaffold accident during the business readjustment, and it’s been going on a little over a year now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the past year where your symptoms went away completely?\n\nPatient: No, not completely. I keep thinking I should’ve seen one more risk coming, even though people tell me the controls were in place. The sailing races, the historical society shift, working in the woodshop… they used to help keep me steady, and now they mostly just feel like tasks I’m avoiding.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel happy or excited about anything at all?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. My wife and kids can be laughing at dinner, or I can finish some difficult milestone at work, and I know those are things that should feel good. But most of the time I just feel kind of flat… like I’m watching it from a little distance instead of really feeling happy or excited.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Since the scaffold accident, during that business readjustment. So… a little over a year now, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or things that you still find pleasure in, no matter how small they might seem?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something, but honestly… not really. The things that used to keep me balanced—sailing races, my Saturday shift at the historical society, even tinkering in the woodshop—they just feel like chores now. I keep putting them off instead of looking forward to them.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of dread and guilt, and the loss of interest in activities, been constant over the past year or have you had periods where you felt better?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty constant, I’d say. I don’t really remember any stretch where I felt back to normal. Since that scaffold accident during the business readjustment… it’s just been there, a little over a year now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past year where you felt a little better and actually enjoyed something?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I might get through a day a little easier here or there, but actually enjoying something… I can’t say I have. That’s what finally got me to come in, honestly. Sailing, the historical society on Saturdays, working in the woodshop—those used to help me feel steady, and now they just feel like chores I keep avoiding.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in enjoyment when spending time with your wife and kids, or do you still find pleasure in being with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that. I still love them, of course, and I want to be there, but it’s different. My wife and kids can be laughing at dinner, and I know I should feel happy in that moment, but mostly I just feel kind of flat, like I’m watching it from a distance. Same with work—if I finish a hard milestone, I know it should feel good, but it just doesn’t really land.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how you've been feeling over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Not much change, honestly. It’s been pretty much the same since the scaffold accident during that business readjustment—so, a little over a year now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your feelings of dread and guilt have gotten worse over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say they’ve stayed with me more than gotten sharply worse. It’s this low-level dread and guilt that follows me around. I keep thinking I should’ve seen one more risk coming, even though people tell me the controls were in place. It’s just hard to let that go.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your feelings over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: Over the past few weeks, not really, no. It’s been about the same. This all kind of started after the scaffold accident during the business readjustment, and it’s been going on a little over a year now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the past year where your symptoms went away completely?\n\nPatient: No, not completely. It’s been going on since that scaffold accident during the business readjustment… a little over a year now. Some days are quieter than others, but I can’t say it ever fully went away.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of dread and guilt increased in intensity over the past year?\n\nPatient: Not really increased, no. It’s more that it’s stayed there — this low-grade dread and guilt that follows me around. I keep feeling like I should’ve seen one more risk coming, even though people keep telling me the controls were in place.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your feelings over the past month?\n\nPatient: Not much change over the past month.
